4 A TREATISE os LEAD.

Etmuller, Fabricius Ab Aqua Pendente, Gui
de Chauliac, Lemeri, Stahl, Mr. Deidier
profeſſor at Montpellier, and by a thouſand
others, whoſe authorities muſt have great
weight ?

Encouraged by ſo many reputable writers,
but convinced ſtill more by my own expe-
rience, I found, in the courſe of many years
practice, that the Extract of Saturn was much
wanted in ſurgery, The many different
forms I have given this medicine render it
very proper for a variety of external com-
plaints. I ſhould with difficulty have ad-
vanced that it had ſucceeded in ſome internal
ones, had not the cures been performed in
the preſence, and under the inſpection, of
many celebrated practitioners, both in ſurgery
and phyſic, at Montpellier; an account of
which I ſhall give in the ſequel. I am well
convinced, that in general, the internal uſe of
Lead is condemned; but may it not be de-
prived of its noxious quality by certain pre-
parations, and adminiſtered in ſmall doles ?
Does not Mr. Van Swieten (an author of
great repute in the preſent age) give inter-
nally the corroſive ſublimate with great ſuc-
ceſs? But I wil fay no more on this head,
my deſign being only to ſhew its good effedts
in external complaints. | N

My own experience, conjointly with that of
many of the Faculty, gives a ſanction to this
remedy ; proving it at the ſame time to be

inſinitely ſuperior to many in uſe, The faQs *
N e | are

INTRODUCTION. 5

are indiſputable; the Public is well ac-
quainted with them; and the unte Hoſpt-
tal at Montpellier for the venereal diſeaſe has
afforded me ſo many opportunities of making
repeated experiments of this medicine, that
the bare recital of the cures it has performed,
would furniſh matter for many volumes.

I cannot conccal the ſatisfaction J have felt,
in being eye-witneſs of the gradual progreſs
the Extract of Lead has made for ſome years
paſt ; the moſt ſkilful practitioners encourage
its uſe ; and even the Miniſtry give it their
ſanction. Mr. Imbert, chancellor of the uni-
verſity, inſpector-general of the hoſpitals of
Provence, Languedoc, and Rouſſillon, was
commiſſioned, by Letters from the Marquis
du Paulmy, ſecretary at war, bearing date the
27th of Ottotter, and the 8th of December,
1756, and the 18th of January 1757, to or-
der various experiments to be made of this
Extract, in the hoſpitals under his inſpection.
This miniſter adds, that in the hoſpitals where
it was tried, it met with general approbation.

Mr. Imbert never publiſhed the ſucceſs of
his experiments; but he aſſured me, that he

ad given the Court a very favourable ac-

r. The Extract of Saturn, mixed with

water, conſtitutes a liquor, which I ſhall

call Vegeto- mineral : this is very proper to

calm all forts of inflammations, and cure

all diforders of the kin, viz. the itch, tet-

ters, &c.
This liquor may very well be ſubſlituted

in the place of wine and brandy, (in every

Eaſe where theſe are generally uſed) as likewiſe

in that of different embrocations, commonly
preſcribed for inflammations ; and more par-
ticularly ſuch as attend gun ſhot wounds: it
is likewiſe very proper for contuſions, bruiſes,
ſprains, echymoſes; for the healing of ulcers,
whether fc: > s, venereal, or cancerous,

to be applied either by injection, fomentation,
or bath. We are not even afraid of afſert-

ing, that this liquor may be ſucceſsfully uſed

to ſtop a gangrene.
In fine, an uninterrupted ſeries of mobil
(when made uſe of in internal inflammations)
as ſufficiently proved, that it ought to be

preferred to emollient decoctions, poultices,

and many other remedies, generally pre-
ſcribed, which too often prove contrary to
the nature of the diſeaſe. Ol this I hope to
convince my readers in the chapter on In-
flammation.

ren ud NW ; boos . 2
Here I* cannot avoid making a reflection,

which ſeems proper to illuſtrate the many

'virtues which a conſtant and ſucceſsful prac-

tice of many years has convinced me are to
be found in Lead, and its different prepara-
tions. The antients, who practiſed more
from their own obſervations and experience
than we do, ſeldom left out ſome preparation
of Lead in. their preſcriptions for external
complaints. This the different diſpenſatories
ſufficiently prove. Mr. Faget, an eminent
Paris ſurgeon, has found the tripharmacum
to be almoſt a ſovereign remedy for many
chirurgical diſorders. Now it is well known
that this plaiſter, which is deſcribed word for
word by Bauderon, and was, before him,
mentioned by Melne, has litharge for its baſis,
and to that owes its efficacy. How then
will it be poſſible for any one, conſidering
the general approbation this metal has met
with for many years paſt, not to find in it
a quality never hitherto diſcovered in any
other? In effect, where is a medicine to be
found, v hoſe particles are at the ſame time
mil, ponderous, flexible, and, by my prepa-
rations of it, ſo ſubdivided as to be capable of
being introduced into the moſt minute vel-
ſels; whoſe texture is ſo fine, and whoſe com-
pactibility is ſo great? I mean that of the
nerves, tendons, aponeuroſes, membranes, &c.
preſerving at the ſame time their own activity
on the blood and lymphatic veſſels, reſtoring
inflamed parts, ſwelled and ſchirrous glands,
— 9 : to

to their natural tone, removing, obſtxuc-

turn that diſperſing and penetrating quality,

dical preſcriptions, and that my own Obler-

On. external” Inflammations ; and of the Action of
e Extratt of Saturn on-theſe Diſorders.

N inflammation of the external parts of
the body is a very common diſeaſe,
and may proceed from a thouſand different
cauſes; ſuch as a blow, a fall, a contuſion,

Kc. from external accident: internally from

any local defect of the ſolids; from the con-

traction of the ſmaller veſſels; from their
ſpaſm ; from their compreſſion, conſtriction,

and obſtruction; from the difficulty which

the blood finds in paſſing the extremity of the
- arteries; from its too great quantity, thick-
neſs, viſcidity, mixture with heterogeneous
and virulent parts; or from any other alter-

ation, whether ſimple or combined, &c.

This diſorder attacks indiſcriminately all

the parts of the human frame which are com-
poſed of ſanguineous and lymphatic veſſels.
Thus in regard to the external parts, nothing
is fo common as inflammations of the ſkin,

fat, glands,” muſcles, membranes, tendons,
'gponeuroſes, and of all the organs which-fall
nder the ſenſes. N

contrary, frequently with violent ones. This

What general diſorder are not the principal
economical and animal functions thrown
into by an inflammation of an aponeuroſis,
or of a tendon ? Nay, convullions, faintings,
eyen death itſelf, are but too often the fatal
conſequences, What is not to be appre-
hended from an inflammation which tends to
a mortification ; eſpecially if deeply lodged
in the internal parts, or attacks a bad ſubject ?

Theſe conſiderations and a thouſand others,

of which it is not my intention to give a detail

in this work, ought to make the ſurgeon ſen-
ſible, how very neceſſary it is for him to be
well acquainted with the remedies that are
proper for the ſubduing of inflammations.

J heſe very remedies are in every one's.
hands, and the moſt ignorant may bluſh not.
to be acquainted with them. But do they
deſerve thoſe praiſes, and that degree of con-
fidence, which is beſtowed upon them ? This.
is a queſtion which their effects alone can de-
cide. 13

dinous and greaſy ſubſtances, waich ſtuff N

decoCtions, conſumed ſo great a:quantity of

was encamped: the inconvenience which re-

in almoſt all the gun-ſhot wounds, which he
ought to have ſtimulated and recalled to life,
inſtead of accelerating the mortification by
emollients. This fact, which makes no apo-
logy for emollitent and relaxing medicines,
was atteeſted to me by many of the profeſ-
fion, who attended the army at that time.

I cannot then help being ſurpriſed that

of late years, and ſo few of the Faculty

In the Medicinal Journal of June 1738,
there is an intereſting letter, addreſſed to
Mr. Vaudermonde by Mr. Boucher, a cele-
brated phyſician of Liſle in Flanders, who,
after having unſucceſsfully tried all kinds of
medicines. to ſubdue a putrid quinſey, which
depopulated the environs of that city, deter-
mined at laſt to make uſe of the ſalt of Lead
in one of theſe caſes, which he had under his
inſpection. The mortified cruſts had ſo far
prevailed, as to cover a great part of the
tonſils, and the anterior pillars of their parti-
tion. To this end, he mixed four-and-twenty

grains of the ſalt of Lead in two ounces of
plantain-water, with which he touched the
cruſts five or fix times a day, with a pencil
made of lint. This remedy, ſays Mr. Bou-
cher, not only prevented the diſeaſe from gain-
ing ground, but the ulcers were daily obſerved BY
to contract, and the eighth day conſolidated ;
and conſequently what remained of the dil-

eaſe was eaſily conquered,

leſs degree of ſenſibility, bear equally the

62 A TREATISE on LEAD,

The bad effects of wounds from fire- arms
may be conſidered in three different points of
view. 1. If any part of our body is injured
by an exploſion of powder, nothing but a
burn will enſue, which will be more or leſs
violent, according to the quantity of powder
exploded, the force with which it has ſtruck
and penetrated the parts, and the extent and
nature of the parts burnt. 2 Balls, or any
other body, ſent out of fire-arms, do not al-
ways cauſe wounds, but ſometimes only
contuſions, which are in general much to be
feared, whether they are con':dered only in
themſelves, or with reſpect to the ſhock and
commotion, which are often communicated
from the contuſed part to the whole frame.

. The violence with which fire-arms expel
the ſolid bodies pent up in them, may enable
us eaſily to comprehend why the wounds
which follow them are ſo frequent. A view
alone of theſe wounds 1s furncient to prove
how dangerous they are in their nature: their
lips and ſurface are not ſmooth and uniform,
as in thoſe made by a ſharp inſtrument, bu:
the fleſh is bruiſed, unequally tore, and
Jagged. Put it would detain us from our
preſent ſubject, to give a long detail of the
difference, ſymptoms, complications, and ct:
fects of theſe diſeaſes.

Now it is very certain, that nothing is lels

fixed, or more undecided, than the methods

which have been and are now uſed in the

treatment of gun-ſhot wounds, Oy as
1.

The Suppuration of every part is generally
preceded by a greater or lels degree of in-
flammation; and by this, chiefly, is a judge
ment to be formed of the violence of the
Suppuration.

Although it has been ſaid, with a great
deal of reaſon, that Suppuration 1s the work
of Nature, it is not leſs true, that it may be
retarded or promoted by art, according to
the choice which 1s made of the medicines
proper for the occaſion. This choice is not
ſo indifferent as is generally imagined, and
often gives riſe to many inconveniences,
This ſubject being lo very important, re-
quires our particular attention.

i-mollients, on which we ſufficiently dwel-

cloler examination wil: prove to us the inſuf-

But to attempt to perſuade the generality

ok Curgeons not to overwhelm, if 1 may ſo

ſpeak, an inflamed part, which tends to up-

theſe facts, though in appearance very ſur.
priſing, becauſe they are confirmed by in-
numerable exp:riments. It may therefore
be looked upon as a truth, that the Extract
of Saturn, applied in its different forms on
the ſuppurated part, penetrates to the mat-
ter, and by a very ſingular action cauſes,
through the pores of the ſkin a diſcharge of
pus, which is found upon the different drel-
fings; by which means, without coming to
any operation, the ſaid pus is by degrees dif-
perſed, and the patient perfectly cured. No
other reaſon can be given for this phænome-
non, than by admitting the metallic particles
to have a peculiar power of dilating the pores
of the ſolid parts which ſurround the mat—
ter, and ſo far attack and attenuate it, as to
render it capable of paſſing through thele
pores. A point of this importance requires
ſome attention in reiterating the experiments,
which have ſo well ſucceeded with me; and
we cannot doubt of the efficacy of this ap-
plication in inflammatory cales not yet

ration. |

bloody and corroſive ſuppurations ; I cannot

By following this method, I have not only
cured the moſt obſtinate and inveterate ul-
cers, but many others of the Profeſſion have
met with equal ſucceſs, as will be ſeen in the
Obſervations. But I muſt here anticipate an
objection that will neceſſarily be made me,
and of which I am ſenſible myſelf.

1 here are ulcers, which are looked upon
as drains, by which the blood 1s purified ; and
which give riſe, when attempted to be cured,
to a reflux of vicious matter into the mals,
the conſequence of which is much more to be
dreaded than the ulcer itſelf. This difficulty,
being founded upon experience, appears at
firſt unanſwerable; however, I dare affirm
that it makes nothing againſt the method
which I practiſe. It is certain, in the treat-
ment of venereal ulcers with medicines that
have not an anti-venereal quality, we only
obtain an illuſory cure; and though we make
the ulcer diſappear, we cannot eradicate the
cauſe, It 1s equally certain, that irritating
applications, repellers, and others of that na-
ture, cannot contribute to heal old ulcers,

without

EXTERNAL SUPPURATIONS, &c. 75

without giving occaſion to a reflux of vicious
matter into the blood ; and I am well per-
ſuaded, that but a very little of the offenſive

and corroſive matter muſt produce the worſt
effects. But the reproaches which theſe re-

medies we have been ſpeaking of ſo juſtly
merit, cannot be given to our metallic one.
It does not act by repulſion, nor does it oc-
caſion any reflux of matter, as it ſets in mo-
tion the ſtagnated humours which choaked
up the edges of the ulcer, and produces an
external ſuppuration; it gently ſtimulates
the ſound fleſh, adds to its ſpring, and
cleanſes, at the ſame time that it blunts, the
acrimony of bad ſuppurations. Thus the
pains are abated, and all the bad ſymptoms,
which generally attend malignant ulcers,
which it effectually cures, by deſtroying the
cauſe, which | ſuppoſe To E LOCATI. What
I have here advanced is founded upon fifteen
years experience; ſo I think it may be de-
pended upon. 3
However, I do not pretend to baniſh, in
the treatment of ulcers, the general or parti-
cular remedies proper to deſtroy the viru-
lence that may be found in the blood, and
which is often the cauſe of bad ſuppurations.
But in general it is worth while remarking,
that old and callous ulcers are commonly
produced by the neglect of ſome inconſider-
able ſore at firſt, or of ſome inflammation
that had come to ſuppuration ; which by bad
treatment often degenerates into an incur-

OBSERVATION I.

Communicated by Mr. EsPIN As.

Mx. DURAND, a cutler of this town, went
to Mr. Eſpinas, a pupil of mine, to aſſuage the
violent pains of an ulcer, which extended from the
middle of his leg to the lower part of the mallco-
Ius internus; an inſupportable cadaverous ftench
iſſued from it, and the edges were hard, callous,
and turned back; the whole was of a livid colour,
bordered upon black : the patient had been trou-
bled with it for forty years, during which time
many ſurgeons had undertaken the cure. The vio-
lence of the pain did not ſuffer him to enjoy any
eaſe day or night; he was incapable of any exer-
ciſe, and never quitted his bed, but to be carried
into his chair, Mr, Eſpinas, who had daily
been a witneſs of the good effects of our remedy,
in the Military Hoſpital, did not heſitate to make
nſe of it. He dreſſed the patient after the manner
already deſcribed; in three hours time the pain
was abated, and in four-and- twenty hours gone off.
The ſmell of the pus changed by degrees, and

ceaſed to be offenſiye. In ſhort, in leis than two

O00S54KyATION I.

ONE METAN, a ſervant, came to conſult me
about a ſcrophulons, ſordid, and fetid ulcer, which
he had under his right ear, I looked upon the
ulcer as venereal, and, in conſequence, ordered
him to bathe it, and uſe ee fe frictions;
which, inſtead of relieving, increaſed the diſorder.
This ulcer continued to corrode the adjacent parts
it penetrated to the internal part of the car, and
the cheek of the ſame fide: he was dreiicd with
our Cerate, and the Vegeto-mineral Water, with
which the ulcerated part was waihed many tines
a-day, over which compreſſes weie laid wetted in
the ſame. This remedy diminiſhed the pain,
but not the ulcer; upon which the patient was

obliged to go to Bareges; from whence he re—
— after a long tay, without being cured,

Upon his arrival at Montpellier, he came again to

conſult me. I told him that my remedy w as beſt
adapted to his caſe, but at the fame time, that great
attention was to be paid to the dreflings, which
ought to be very frequent. By following my ad-
vice, he had the ſatisfaction of ſeeing the ulcer
gradually diminiſh ; and in fix weeks time he was
perfectly cured.

Since the writing of this Obſervation, many
ulcers have appeared in different parts of his bod dy,
as upon the ſhoulder, neck, and head; in which
part the bones are diſcoverable for a conſiderable
extent. Our medicine ey effected the cure of
this evil, although unaſſiſted by a regular regimen,
It is true that a ſmall ulcer yet remains, and many
more are to be apprehended ; but at the ſource of

OBSERVATION III.
ONE ANTHONY FAB RE, a ſoldier in

the royal regiment of cavalry, came to the hoſpital
in the year 1756, to be cured of two ulcers; one
of which was ſeated on the poſterior and middle
part of his right leg, the other on the upper and
internal part of his right hand,

- Theſe ulcers were of the moſt malignant kind,
with very callous edges; and the ſuppuration was
of ſo bad a quality, that it corroded all the neigh-
bouring parts: that on the leg had deſtroyed part
of the muſculi gemini; and the one on the hand,
the muſculi interoſſei. The matter had penetrated
through the hand; the flexor tendons of the fingers
were laid bare; the patient ſuffered excruciating
pains; and the whole was horrible to the eye.
He told us, that ſuch had been his ſituation for
two years paſt, which gave reaſon to ſuſpeCt that
it proceeded from a ſcrophulous habit of body:
many ſurgeons had undertaken his cure; and his
pittance being ſpent in ſearch of it, he reſolved to
get into the Hoſpital,

I ordered him to be dreſſed according to my
nſual method: the ulcer was injected with our
liquor, and the pledgets covered with the Cerate ;
the compreſles, dipped in the Vegeto-mineral
Water, were often moiſtened, and the dreſſings
conſtantly changed twice a-day. In eight days
there was a great change for the better; but the
patient having given ſome reaſon to ſuſpect, that
they might have been produced by a venerea

OBSERVATION V.
A SOLDIER of the regiment of Burgundy

deſcribed, the ulcer was perfectly cured in a very
ſhort time.

OBSERVATION XIII.

Communicated by Mr. DipiER, Surgeon-major of the
Corſican Regiment of Cavalry.

A SOLDIER, who had a carious ulcer on
the leg, has been cured by the Extract of Saturn,
after every other medicine which could be thought
of had failed. Being well convinced that my pa-
tient had no bad humour in his blood, I put him
under a proper regimen, and ordercd him to keep
his bed; I purged him from time to time, and
took care to remove all the proud fleſh from the
fore, which I waſhed three or four times a-day
with the Extract, modified according to the ſtate
of the ulcer'; whoſe aſpe& was hideous, when [I

OBSERVATION XV.

Communicated by Mr. BRUGUIERE, Surgeon-majer of
the Regiment of Tour-du-pin,

A SOLDIER of the regiment of Mailly had
been troubled for four or five years paſt, with a
cancerous ulcer, which occupied all the anterior
part of the leg, with a tumefaction of the middle
part of the tibia. I gave him a bottle of the Ve-
geto-mineral Water, and deſired him to wet the
compreſſes, when he found them dry; and, as he
had the advantage of being near a ſtove, he warm-
ed the water. My patient followed 5 my
orders; in about four or five days, I perceived the
edges of the ulcer much cleaner, the bottom red,
and the vacancies filled up with good fleſh; and
that, by perſeverance, he was in a fair way of
being cured, In fine, after a month's application
of this medicine, he was not only freed from this
ujcer, but likewiſe from the tumefaction of the
tibia, Of this fact Mr, Laroque, phyſician to the
army and to the hoſpital at Duiſbourg, was an eye-
witneſs, This phyfician could not help mentioning
the cure; and atteſted it by a memoir, which I ſent
to Monſ. De Paulmy, then Secretary at War, who
did me the honour to write me a letter upon the
occaſion, which I {till preſerve.

I was not afraid of advancing in the pre-
lude to this Chapter, that my medicine had
often proved ſucceſsful in diſperſing abſceſſes,
after they had ſuppurated. The following
Obſervations wall 200 to prove what I have
laid upon the occaſion,

G. HAF. IV.

On Cancers, whether occult or ulcerated; and
of the Action of the Extract of Saturn againſe
theſe Complaints,

T is a received opinion among Phyſicians
and Surgeons, that external applic ations

are uleleſs, and generally dangerous, in the
treatment of cancerous tumours. This opi-
nion is adopted by practitioners of the higheſt
reputation. Mr. Van Swieten, in his Com-
mentaries on the Aphoriſms of Boerhaave,
declares himſelf of Hildanus's opinion, whoſe
Obſervations prove, that plaiſters of muci-
lage and melilot, mild as they are, augment
the redneſs and pain of a Cancer, as often as
applied. This Obſervation, ſays the illuſtri-
ous Commentator, juſtifies the Aphoriſm of
Hippocrates, who teaches us not to attempt
the cure of occult Cancers, We likewile
find in Hildanus, and in other Obſervations,
a number of examples which confirm the
truth of Hippocrates's opinion,

This opinion, fo univerſally adopted, is the
cauſe of theſe unhappy ſufferers being left a
ſacrifice to the moſt horrible pains, w -hich no
medicine can calm, and which frequently
conduct them to their graves, From hence

But we are not to neglect internal remedies

ONE JANE DE RIUSSE, wife of Vede!, 2

had been hitherto free, now began to torment her,

mineral Water, which I ordered her to renew three

and the regimen I had preſcribed, I perceived a

From this Obſervation, joined with man
others, conſequences may be drawn of the
molt beneficial kind; for they ſerve to prove
by experiments, how much thoſe are, and
have been to blame, who forbid external ap-
plications on Cancers, whether in their com-
mencement, or after they are confirmed.
But the greateſt advantages of all are their
methods of cure. We cannot ſay too much
to prove our aſſertions, ſeeing our opinion
contradicts that of the greateſt men; and
that it is by Obſervations and reiterated ex-
periments only, we can bring others to the
lame way of thinking with ourſelves.

A SOLDIER, of the regiment of Burgundy,
came to the Hotel Dieu at Montpellier, afflicted
with a hard inflammatory tumour on the poſterior
and lower part of the right thigh; it extended
three or four fingers breadth above the ham, and
about an inch below it. The ſurgeon of the ward
examined the tumour, and ſeeing it did not tend
to reſolution, he applied a cataplaſm of bread and
water. This was continued for four days without
any viſible alteration, as it neither diſperſed, nor
looked as if it would come to ſuppuration: on the
contrary, it rather ſeemed to grow harder, which
determined him to apply reſolvents. He then or-
dered a plaiſter of Vigo, mucilage and diabotanum, .

This

os A TREATISE ov LEAD.

This was continued for eight days without any di-
minution of the tumour, which he likewiſe pumped
with bran-water : but all was ineffectual. A cata-
plaſm was then applied of bread and Vegeto- mineral
Water, which had ſo good an effect, that in a
weck's time the tumour was ſo far diſperſed, that
there was not the leaſt mark of it remaining.

„„

On Sprains, Stiffneſs of the Joints, and Relax-
ation of the Ligaments; and of the Ef of the
Extrad of Sajurn on thefe Diforders.

1. Ctifineſs of the joints may be occa-
toned by a redundancy or thickneſs
of iynovia, or that fluid which Nature has
provided to keep them ſupple. This may
proceed from various caules, both external
and internal. I ſhall not enter into a detail
of them, as the only object I have in view is
to mention ſuch facts as may prove of ſervice
to the Gentlemen of the Profeſſion and their
aticnts.
it is allowed, on all hands, that a redun-
dancy of this fluid, when once fixed, is not
ealily removed ; and it is equally certain, that
the applications hitherto made uſe of to dil-
perſe it, have often proved uſeleſs : and that
in courſe of time, its haxdneſs and conſiſt-

ency render the diſeaſe ablolutely incurable.
Our

538

CP RAINS. 97

Our application 1s the true ſpecific for eſ-
fecting a reſolution of this kind of tumours,
eſpecially in the beginning. In the courſe
of a long practice, I have always experienced
it to be effectual in caſes where this fluid
was thickened, whether in the joints or in-
volucra of the tendons, even when the ſharp-
nels of the humour had occaſioned gouty
ſwellings. However, what I have advanced
is not grounded on my own Obſervations
only, but on thoſe of many practitioners; as
will afterwards appear. Before I. enter upon
the facts which I have to mention, I think
myſelf obliged to declare, that we owe to the
Mareſchal de Richelieu the perfection of the
remedy which we employ for the cure of ſuch
diſorders in the joints as are occaſioned by
the thickneſs of the fluid lodged in their cavi-
tes. The addition of ſoap to the other ingre-
dients of the Pomatum, of which we ſhall
give an account in its proper place, was in-
vented at Genoa by that illuſtrious nobleman.
It was with this Pomatum I made a cure, in
the year 1748, of which I ſhall give a detail,
and which 1s one of the moſt ſurpriſing of the
kind that was ever performed.

2. Our remedy is allo admirable in all caſes
where the ligaments of the articulations are
relaxed, whether the cauſe which produced
that relaxation be external or internal, gene-
ral or particular, &c.

OBSERVATION IX.
Tur CHEVALIER DESQUILLES, ſon of

the Marquis De Meſplex, of Pau in Bearn, page
iv the Duke of Orleans, being at play with his
companions, in one of the rooms of the Palais
Royal (this room was parquetted and waxed over)
made a conſiderable effort by an extention of his
legs, the one from the other. The ligaments
Which confine the head of the femur in its ſocket
received ſo confiderable an injury, that the pa-
vent, through the exceſſive pain he felt, was oblig-
ed to be carried up to his chamber. Meſſieurs Fa-
get and Marſolan, two eminent ſurgeons, were
lent for; nor was any thing ſpared to give their

3 patient

102 A TREATISE on LEAD

patient eaſe z they attended him for above fix
months without ſucceſs; and as his pains conti-
nued to increaſe, it was thought proper to ſend
him to Bareges, At firſt, theſe waters ſeemed to
relieve him; he then went to his father's houſe,
when his pains, during the winter, returned upon
him with double violence. This determined him
to have recourſe in the ſpring to the ſaid waters;
but they only ſerved to add to his ſufferings ; upon
which he was carried to Montpellier in the yer
1749. A conſultation was there held of Mel-
ſieurs Lazermés and Serane, phyſicians, and Mr,
Lamorier, my confrere, and myſelf, The follow-
ing is an account of his ſituation : When he lay
down, the diſeaſed thigh was longer than the
other; it was continually bent, and could by no
means be moved, without cauſing great pains in
the flexor muſcles. The part was cold, and it was
with difficulty that any warmth could be com-
municated to it, The patient could not move it
without afliſtance, and even then felt excruciating
pains both in that part and the leg; he was unable
to put his foot to the ground, as he was to wats
without crutches, Having examined the part, we
found the head of the femur had been forced out
of its acetabulum or cavity, without being above
its level: this was apparent from the inequality of
length in the limbs, eſpecially when the patient
was put into certain poſitions, It muſt likewiſe be
obſerved, that the part was conſiderably waſted. |
We conſulted what was beſt to be done for him
in this fituation : all preſent declared him inch.
rable; notwithſtanding, it was vaguely propose
that he might try the baths of Balaruc : he was at-
tended by a ſurgeon of his own ; who deſpaires,
like the reſt, of his recovery. Upon this, I pro-
poſed a trial of my remedies ; and the ſurgeon con

tented, having orders from the patient's father gr

OBSERVATION X.

Communicated,

Maname DE LA GOMERCINT, a Genoeſe
lady, had been troubled from her childhood with
a relaxation of the capſular ligaments of her left
thigh, The diforder had been increafing for fif—
teen years paſt; and ſo much ſo, that the motion
of the part was inſenſibly leſſened. The weaknels
was ſo great, that ſhe was unable to ſupport her-
ſelf; as the likewiſe was either to walk, fit down,
or get up without aſſiſtance. Though the diſor—
der had originally been of a long ſtanding, it was
only for the fix or ſeven Jaſt years that it had made
any conſiderable progreſs, When I had the ho-
nour of attending her, in company with two Ge-
noeſe phyſicians, I found her pains exceſſive, and
the whole thigh conſiderably emaciated, For many
years paſt, this lady had conſulted the moſt emi-
nent of the Faculty, in different parts of the world,
had made trials of various baths, and of many other
remedies, without finding advantege from any.
Having carefully examined the part, I found, that
the head of the femur was diſplaced, and lodged
upon the muſcles of the buttock, which made that
limb ſhorter than the other by about four fingers

breadth, I concluded, after having examined the

grievance, that by a relaxation of the ligaments of
the part, the muſcle of the buttock had contracted
and drawn the head of the femur from its cavity
upwards ; and that this, by preſſing on the poſte-
rior ſciatique nerve, gave riſe to the pains my pa-
tient felt. My opinion was, that ſhe muſt inevit-
ably remain a cripple, and that the limb would at
laſt wither away; and, in fine, that the only me-
thod remaining to give her eaſe, would be to re-
place the head of the femur in its acetabulum : To
effect which, I took the following method:
ordered two jugs to be filled with the Vegeto-
mineral Water warm: one affiſtant held the
trunk, another the lower part of the limb, who
cently moved it backwards and forwards ; in the
mean time, a third poured from the jug the Vegeto-
mincral Water upon the part, whilſt a fourth was
employed in rubbing it. It was not long before
the lady found the good effects of this operation.
The head of the bone was ſoon brought upon a
level with its cavity, and in leſs than fifteen days
re-cntcred it. My patient was then able to ſupport
herſelf, and walk, I took care to apply one of my
daturnine plaiſters to the grievance, and a bandage
upon that, This lady perſevered in the uſe of my
remedies for above two years, after the bone had
been replaced. Though I have not ſeen her for
a confiderable time, I am well aflured that her
cure has been compleat; ſeldom a year paſſes,
without my hearing of her; and I am informed

that ſhe finds not the leaſt bad effects from her old
complaint.

OBSERVATION XII.

A SAXON nobleman had a fall, which cauſcd
a luxation of the radius, attended with a confider-
able contuſion of the adjacent parts. He was at-
tended for ſome time by many eminent ſurgeons;
but finding he had loſt the uſe of the arm, he did
me the hononr to write to me from Bourdeaux for
my advice, which I ſent him, with my Extract of
Saturn, and the receipt for making my Pomatum
with ſoap. Mr. Giraud, an able ſurgeon of Agen,
informed me, that he had ſeen this gentleman at
Bareges ; and had learned from him, that my me-
dicine had done him great ſervice : this account
the above gentleman confirmed to me in perſon, on
his road to Montpellier.

conſequently the 2 of the fingers. I was
determined to make uſe of my preparations with
the Extract of Saturn; and this the more willingly,
as I had learned, by experience, how very proper
this metallic remedy was to reſolve the inſpiſſated
juices, which choak up the veſſels, and even the
ſynovia in the articulations; as likewiſe to reſtore
weak parts to their proper tone; eſpecially when
loſt by an accident.

OBSERVATION XIV.

On a Retraftion and Hardneſs of the Flexor Muſcles
of the Thigh, accompanied with a confiderable Ten/irn
of the Muſculus Biceps.

A DAUGHTER of one Ayrel, a ſhocmaker,
of about eleven years of age, had her right thigh
ſo contracted, that if any one offered to move it,
however gently, ſhe ſuffered intolerable pains : in
this ſituation ſhe had remained for three months,
and many remedies had been applied without ſuc-
ceſs. She was at laſt brought to me. I thought
that the pumping of the 8 Water
upon the part might be of great uſe to her: nor
was I deceived in my conjectures; for by follow-
ing my advice ſhe found eaſe in leſs than two days;
and on the tenth ſhe was able to Walk about 23
uſual,

IxNCOMPLEAT LUXATIONS of the Knee, 109

from their own diſpoſition, and partly from

the weight of the limb, and contraction of
the muſcles of the part affected; aud if the
bone is not confined in its place by a ban-
dage, the cavities of the joints are filled with
ſynovia, which 1s accumulated and thickened
to ſuch a degree, that it will be a difficult
matter to replace the ſaid bone.

Mr. Petit, in his excellent Treatiſe on the
Diſeaſes of the bones, takes notice, that an
incompleat luxation ſeldom happens to the
articulation of the knee; and this 1s true,
becauſe a round extremity, covered with a
poliſhed and ſmooth cartilage, and oiled with
its proper fluid, cannot remain long upon
the edge of its ſocket. The head of a bone
thus circumſtanced, when driven to the edge
by any external violence, muſt either fall
out or return into its ſocket. Thus, either
there will be no luxations, or it will be com-
pleat ; which 1s far from being the caſe of
ſuch joints of the human body as are formed
like a hinge.

It 1s nevertheleſs true, that the bones of
the joint of the knee are ſometimes diſlocated
by external cauſes in an incompleat manner ;
but this never happens immediately, or at
the moment in which we fall, or receive a
blow : it can only happen in caſes of fails, or
uncommon exertions, which particularly af—
ict the ligaments that are ſtrained and
bruiſed; and diſpoſed to relaxation. The
bone not being fixed exactly in its ſocket,

When the head of the bone has been con-
ducted into its cavity, we ſhould be careful
to give frequent but gentle motion to the
Joint : this motion, often repeated during the
pumping, and aſſiſted by the action of our
remedy, produces ſurpriſing effects; and 1
can venture to ſay, that it is particularly effi-
cacious in acting upon the redundant fluid,
when thickened in any cavity, or the ſheaths
of the tendons, which is the caſe in anchye
loſes. |

In luxations where the ligaments are much
injured, great care muſt be taken not to
harraſs the part too much after the bone is
replaced. The gentleman who makes the
ſubje& of the Ninth Obſervation, would have
had nothing to fear from a relapſe, if he had
not indulged himſelf in violent exerciſes, ſuch

as hunting, dancing, and other movements

unfavourable to the recovery of the tone of
the articular ligaments: he ſhould have al-
lowed more time to the bathing with the
Vegeto-mineral Water, and to the uſe of the
Saturnine plaiſter.

When people advanced in years have the
misfortune to fall, the conſequences are
dreadful. As often as the ligaments of the
joints are affected, the diſeaſe is generally too
violent to be compleatly cured. But even
theſe patients may be eaſed by our remedies,

and by a well-made bandage, which they
mult wear for a long time,

GOUTY and RHEUMATIC PAINS. 113

without putting them to inſupportable tor-

We ſhall not enter into a detail of the diſ-
tant cauſes that produce Rheumatic Pains ;
their cauſes are innumerable : we will only
obſerve, that in the Army, whole intereſt and
advantage we always have in view, theſe dil-
eaſes are often occalicned by a bad regimen 3;
by cold and damps, to which the ſoldiers
are often expoſed ; by too free an indulgence
in the uſe of brandy and other ſpirituous
liquors, &c. But let the caule of Rheu-
matic Pains be what it will, they are never
produced but when the ſerum has contracted
a certain thickneſs or ſharpneſs, or both the
one and the other of theſe cauſes ; which,
when confidered ſeparately, or in a ſtate of
combination, occaſion, 1. A ſlowneſs in the
circulation of the lymph, which moiſtens
the texture of the muſcles, and their ex-
ternal teguments. 2. More or leſs conſi-
derable obſtructions in the lymphatic veſſels
of thele parts. 3. Irritations and vellica-
tions of the nervous fibres which enter their
compoſitions, 4. Laſtly, the other effects
which accompany or follow the above mor-
bic changes.

Practice informs us, that there are Rheu-
matic pains exceedingly obſtinate; amongſt
which, thoſe rheumatiſms that are gouty
or venereal are ſometimes ranked. Some-
mes we alſo meet with infinite difficulties
in the cure of ſimple rheumatiſms; but

It is pretended that the perioſteum is

matic Pains which are attriguted to an injury
of the perioſteum, with thoſe pains that at-

are felt very deep.

In phyfic and ſurgery, diſeaſes to which

ill treated; they often become inveterate,
and laſt not for years only, - but for lite;

great eaſe in theſe pains, eſpecially if the
patients conſtantly lead a regular life. Bath-
ing and pumping with certain mineral waters
often effect cures of rheumatic complaints,
which had been proof many years againſt
all kinds of internal medicines. The me-
dicinal particles of theſe waters are intro-
duced into the pores of the 1{kin, eſpecially
if their paſſage is facilitated by frictions or
pumpings ; they penetrate even to the ob-
ſtructed lymphatic veſſels, where they atte-
nuate and divide the viſcous fluid that ex-
cites the Rheumatic Pains ; this lymph having
thus recovered its natural fluidity, takes
the ordinary courſe of circulation. And as
the acrimony of this humour had been en-
tirely deſtroyed, or very much diminiſhed
by the antecedent uſe of {weeteners, the two
vices which conſtitute rheumatic complaints
are deſtroyed ; and from hence may be dated
a radical cure; which indeed may be effected
by any other medicine, either internal or
external, which by its penetrating quality
will deſtroy viſcidity, and by its ſweetening
quality blunt the acrimony and ſharpneſs of
the lymph.

It would be wandering from our ſubject

neral waters act in the cure of Rheumatiſms ;

The argument formed againſt our remedy,

OBSERVATION.
A POOR LAD, after a long impriſonment,

was ſeized with ſo violent a Rheumatic Pain in bis
left arm, that it appeared paralytic: every remedy
that could be thought of was uſed for three months
ſucceſſively. The intendant's lady ſent for me, and
deſired ] would attend him. I ordered his arm to
be rubbed with the Vegeto-mineral Water warm;
I then applied one of my Saturnine plailters, and
a warm cloth over that. This was continued
twice a day; in about eight days he was much te-
lieved, and in ten or twelve was perfeQly cured.
The ſucceſs of this medicine was the more extfa-

ordinary, as the arm appeared paralytic and mo-

OBSERVATION III.

A SOLDIER of the regiment of Briſſac came to
our Hoſpital, afflicted with ſo violent a pain in
his right-arm, that neither bleeding nor narcotic
medicines could remove it, He was treated as
aboye, and was very ſoon cured.

OBSERVATION IV.
A SOLDIER of the Lyoneſe regiment had

ſuch violent pains in the articulations of the lower
extremities of the right-ſide, that he was unable
to walk without crutches. Every remedy that
could be thought of had been tried before in St.
Liizs's Hoſpital 5 upon which he was ſent to ours,
as it was conjectured they might proceed from a
venereal cauſe, But mercury was adminiſtered
Without effect: his ſituation continued the ſame,
and he was unable to move. We applied our me-
dicine, as we had done to the former, which gave

him almoſt immediate relief; and, in fine, radi-

cally cured him in a few days.

OBSERVATION. VIII.
THE DAUGHTER of a ſhocmaker, who lives

in the ſuburbs of this town, of about ten or eleven
years of age, was afflicted laſt ſummer with a con-
traction of the muſcles of the thigh, and of thoſe
of the leg of the ſame fide, Her parents had con-
ſulted many of the Faculty, and particularly Mr.
Leroi, profeſſor of phyſic. Different remedies were
preſcribed without ſucceſs, which made them fear
ſhe would become a cripple, In fine, ſhe was
brought to me; and upon examination I was of
opinion, that the part affected ſhould be bathed
with the Vegeto-mineral Water warm, and then
covered up with warm cloths. This was accord-
ingly done twice a-day : and in about ten or twelve
days ſhe was ſo well recovered, as to be in the
ſtrects with her mother; where I met her, nor
without ſome degrec of ſurpriſe.

ETTERS may, in general, be

looked upon either as affections pure.
ly local, or as diſeaſes that are occaſioned by
a vitious quality of the general maſs of the
blood and lymph. The firſt ſpecies pre-
ſuppoſes a morbific change in ſome part «f
the texture of the ſkin, in conſequence of
which the humours ſtop, and afterwards ac-
quire more or leſs acrimony and viſcidity;
the others eſſentially depend upon a part:
cular depravation of the humours, and prin-
cipally of thoſe that are lymphatic. Thu
ſecond ſpecies of Tetters is often hereditary,
and more difficult to cure than the fil;
the morbific cauſe being not leſs general than

it is in the venereal diſeaſe, ſcurvy, king in the
evil, &c, With, in
This cauſe once ſuppoſed, it will be very the num
eiſy to explain, why Tetters indiſtinctly ap- them, tl
pear in every part of the body; why they

are fixed ſometimes to one part, and ſome- Fee
times paſs from one place to another; wh oners, tþ
having once diſappeared, either by the cli® produce
of medicine or ſimple efforts of nature, he blog,
they return again when they are leaſt W

liquors have been taken too freely, &c.

My deſign is not to enter here into a the-
oretic detail of the different kinds of Tetters,
or of all the cauſes which produce them,
and the marks by which they are diſtin-
guiſhed ; 1 only mean to inſinuate that theſe
cutaneous eruptions, whether ſimple or com-
plicated, or even hereditary, may not only
be undertaken, but radically cured, with-
out the danger of thoſe accidents which are
magnified by a ſet of practitioners, who
are pleaſed to ſtile this diſeaſe Incurable,
My remedy prevents every miſchievous ac-
cident in the cure of Tetters, let them be
dry, moiſt, pimpled; ſcabby, cruſty, &c. It
s a fact, of which I am convinced by a
practice of between fifteen and eighteen
years, particularly in the Royal Hoſpital,
Where, during the ſpace of more than ten
years, I have met with the greateſt ſucceſs,
in the treatment of all Tetters that I met
with, in my practice there; though, amongſt
the numbers of thoſe that I ſaw afflicted with

lepers, | |

Tetters are in general very troubleſome,
either from the infupportable itchings that
they occaſion, or from an acrimonious ſup-
puration. Thoſe who are afflicted with this
diſeaſe, are much inclined to ſcratch, which
ſo irritates and inflames the ſkin as to pro-
duce reftleſs nights: ſometimes, again, the
parts inflame, ſuppurate, and ulcerate ; and.
it is not at all uncommon to ſee theſe ulcers
become habitual, for the want of proper re-
medies to calm the irritations, and blunt the
acrimony of the purulent matter that runs
from them.

The variety of external applications in the
treatment of Tetters is almoſt infinite: but
| am not afraid to aſſert, that many are
uſeleſs, and others dangerous. To be con-
vinced of this, it 1s ſufficient to perule the
works of practitioners who have treated of
ſurgery, and particularly the Treatiſe of Tur-
ner, who is the beſt writer upon the diſeaſes
of the ſkin. This author preſcribes a number
of remedies ; but there is not one upon which
we can depend for a radical cure of Tetters ;
while it would be extremely dangerous to
make uſe of many which he has recommend-
ed: ſuch are thoſe which are compounded
of vitriol, alum, lime, the ſublimate in pow-
dor, mercury, verdigriſe, white precipitate,
red precipitate, and a number of other in-
gredients, of which the action was never
vell aſcertained, and always to be ſuſpected.

The conclufion to be drawn from the above

ONE VICTOIRE, of the battalion of

cure. By the uſe of domeſtic baths, herb: broths,
and the application of the Vegeto-mineral Water,
and the Pomatum made with the Extract of Saturn,
(ſce the Formule) he was radically cured in feven-

and thirty days.

above- mentioned.

OBSERVATION XIII.

IN the year 1746, Mr. de Montbas, captain,
had ſo violent a humour in his legs, that the Mare-
chal De Belleiſle ordered him to quit the army to
get cured; they were covered with Tetters, that
were both inflamed and ulcerated. Many remedies
had been made uſe of without ſucceſs. On his
arrival at Montpellier, he called a conſultation of
Meſſieurs Lazermes, Serrane, ſenior, and myſelf.
The phyſicians were of opinion, that he ſhould
make uſe of various internal remedies, and go to
Bareges. But upon their leaving the room, I al-
ſured the patient, that if he would ſtay at Mont-
pellier, 1 could be of no ſmall aſſiſtance to him.
Upon hearing this, he reſolved to come to my
houſe, where I gave him the internal remedies
that he had been adviſed to take. I then applied
my Pomatum, and the Vegeto-mineral Water, In
three days there was ſo 2 a change for the bet-
ter, that I defired Mr. Serrane ſenior to come 10
my houſe, to be witneſs of it. This gentleman ex-

reſſed his ſurpriſe to me in very flattering terms.
he patient, by perſevering in the uſe of the above
remedies, was ſoon in a fituation to return home,
there to continue the internal ones. The com-
plaint in his legs was removed; and the year after
I had the honour of ſeeing him at Genoa in perſect

HE Itch is an eruption of little cuta- unguiſſ

The Itch is generally divided into two

kinds; one is called the dry or canine, the

other the moiſt Itch; but theſe two may. be
ſubdivided into four other kinds, which de-

ſerve the particular attention of a judicious

pratitioner. To follow this ſubdiviſion, in-
dicated by Mr. Raimond, in his Treatiſe upon
Diſeaſes that are dangerous to cure, we will
conſider the Itch under four different points
of view: as, critical, ſpontaneous, ſymptomatic,
and communicated. Ihe firſt is the termina-
tion of an acute or chronical diſeaſe, when
the matter is depoſited on the ſurface of the
body. The ſecond appears of itſelf, and is
not preceded by any illneſs, but is produced
by a thickneſs and acrimony of humours con-
trated by a groſs unhealthy diet, and a re-
ſpiration of a moiſt and foul air; it princi-
pally attacks ſea-faring people. The third 1s
an effect or ſympton of another diſeaſe, as
venereal complain:s, jaundice, ſcurvy, &c.
The fourth 1s acquired by touching and dwel-
ling with thoſe who have the Itch.

I ſaid above, that the different kinds of
Itch, of which I have given a ſhort account,
may be moiſt or dry. The dry Itch is dil-
tinguiſhed by ſmall and almoſt imperceptible
pimples, and a very troubleſome itching,
that obliges the patients to ſcratch, whether
they will or not. The moiſt, or the great

Itch, is generally marked by more conſider-

able puſtules, even often greater than are
thoſe of the finall-pox : theſe puſtules are

COm—-

134 ATREATISE ox LEAD,

commonly filled with matter, and cauſe les
itching than 1s felt in the dry or canine Itch,

There is yet another ſpecies of the Itch,
which I have often obſerved : this is a com.
bination of the moiſt Itch and ulcerated and
cruſted Tetters. It ſhews itſelf in blotches,
as large as the palm of the hand, on the arms,
legs, and ſometimes alſo on other parts of
the body. |

1 he feat of the Itch is commonly faid to
be in the glands of the texture of the ſkin,
Mr. Raimond 1s not at all of that opinion;
he thinks that the ſeat of this diſorder is in
the nipples, or nervous pyramides, that form
the organ of touch. But neither does his
{yſtem appear ſufficiently clear, nor his opi-
nion probable.

It will eaſily be perceived, that the prog-
noſtic of this diſeaſe muſt vary according to
the different kinds we have juſt enumerated,
The critical terminates a diſeaſe, when the
morbid matter is entirely depoſited upon the
ſkin, and then does not require any pati!-
cular treatment. The third kind mult be
ranked among the diſeaſes of which it 1s 2
complication ; and with regard to the two
others, that is to ſay, the ſpontaneous Itch,
and that contracted by contagion, we can
aſſert, that they are not all dangerous of
themſelves, but that this 'may become vel)
much ſo, if the matter is imprudently le
pelled: and this is confirmed by many ob-

ſervations collected by authors, and *

farly by Mr. Raimond. It is pretended that

in ſome countries, as in Brittany, the Itch
is endetnical ; and there we are aſſured, that
z number of people retain, during their whole

We come now to the cure, which varies
equally with the prognoſtic, according to
the particular nature of the Itch. Of the
four kinds taken notice of by Mr. Raimond,
there is not one with which our remedy does
not very well agree; ſeeing it invariably pro-
duces the ſame effect, in multiplying the itchy
eruptions, and conſequently cleanſing the
maſs of blood. However, we ſhall ſpeak more
particularly of that Itch which is formed by
the habit, and of that which is contracted by
contact with thoſe infected with it, as theſe
more frequently occur in practice,

Few diſeaſes have had more remedies pre-
ſcribed for them; but Turner's Treatiſe on
Cutaneous Diſorders preſents us with a mul-
tiplicity of applications, which are not eaſily
enumerated, and from which the moſt ſan-
guine practitioners promiſe a dubious ſuc-
cels, Authors of this claſs muſt have hoped
to promote the intereſts of mankind. How-
ever, | ſhall make no apology for affirming,
that the ſtile and method of theſe writers
may poſſibly be as prejudicial to young prac-
titioners, as they are diſagreeable and irkſome
to their readers. The only two ſpecifics for

Another advantage that would reſult from
the medicine which we propoſe, is, that the
eyes would not ſuffer, as they frequently do,
from the forgetfullneſs of the patients, who
are too apt to rub them with hands that
have been beſmeared with ſulphur. What J
have here ſaid of the effects of my remedy is
not exaggerated, ſeeing I can alledge in its
favour more than 2500 Caſes, beſides the ad-
vantageous atteſtation that has been given-
me by Meſheurs Imbert and Fournier. This
alone would be a ſufficient recommendation
of it, if ignorance, ſordid envy, vile intereſt,
and a thouſand other contemptible motives,
did not almoſt always raiſe a clamour againſt
the man, who preſumes to render himſelf
uſefull to his fellow-creatures by a new diſ-
covery, from a ſet of people, who never
having done any thing to deſerve the atten-
tion of the Public; ſeek to emerge from their
obſcurity by railing at the talents of others.
To prevent the malicious imputations of
theſe people, the number of whom is un-
fortunately too great, I ſhall lay before the
reader ſuch peices, as, in the eyes of every.
man of ſenſe, muſt fully juſtify the praiſes I
lave beſtowed upon my remedy, lels from

__ may be obſerved from the annexed ac.
counts, ſays Mr. Lanoy, that ſome patients
have been cured in a very ſhort time, others
more ſlowly, and ſome required bleeding
and purging again. That depends, accord.
ing to the remark of that judicious phyh.
cian, upon the kind and duration of the
Itch: For ſuch as had lately contraQted it,
were cured in a few days; but it proved
more obſtinate in ſuch as had been troubled
with it for ſome months. The ſame ma
be ſaid of thoſe patients who laboured under
ſome other diſeaſe, together with the Itch,
as continual or intermittent fevers : the cure
of theſe was not ſo ſpeedy, and it was ne-
ceſſary to prepare them by the remedies
commonly preſcribed for purifying the
blood; ſuch as decoctions of the roots of
barnada, china root, æthiops mineral, and
ſuch like.

SERVATION V the Phyſician of tbe
92 Hoſpital of 515 3 1

THE uſe which has been made in the Military
Hoſpital of Arras, of a remedy fent down by the
Court under the name of the Extract of Saturn,
tor the cure of the Itch; has had all the good ef-
ſects that could be expected from it; if we except
only a certain number of ſoldiers, on whom it
took no effect; from which there was reaſon to
ſuſpe&, that ſome other diſeaſe might have been
joined to the Itch. Nor was this a mere conjec-
ture; for by the uſe of baths and frictions on the
wriſts and hams with mercurial ointment and
zthiops mineral, a ptiſan of the root of bardana
and lapathum, they were entirely cured,

The preſent account given by me, phyſician to
the ſaid hoſpital. Arras, the 1ſt of Auguſt, 1757.

DuRrannD.

OBSERVATION of the Surgemn-Major of
the Hoſpital at Bethune.

THE well known effects of the Extract of
Saturn perfectly anſwer the author's intention
always ſuppoſing the uſe of it to be preceded by
bleeding and phyſick; as theſe are neceſſary prepa-
rations for diſpoſing the blood to be benefitted by
this remedy, I have obſerved, that of forty pa-
tients with the Itch, who were treated with this
medicine, ſome were cured the 6th, the 8th, and
the toth day; none excerded the 16th. I have
farther ob erved, that they were all cured by a co-
pious perſpiration; and upon that account [ pre-
ferred the adminiſtering it at night, when the pa-
tent went to bed. For it is well known, that the
wo kinds of Itch, both the moiſt and the dry, are
ſeated in the glands of the texture of the ſkin, and
that ys can more conduce to its cure than a

copious per{piration, which is always promoted by
lie warmth of the bed.

138 A TREATISE on LEAD.

The Extract of Saturn is an active, ſearching
medicine; whoſe fine, penetrating particles divide
the itchy concretions, and cauſe them to tranſpire
through the pores of the ſkin, by means of a copi-
ous ſweat, which is brought on after every friction.

It is finally our opinion, that this mnch approved
remedy ſeems the moſt efficacious, and the on
one hitherto diſcovered, that ought to be made ule
of. Such is the reſult of the Obſervations made
upon the effects of that remedy in the Military
Hoſpital at Bethune. Bethune, the 4th of March,
1757. Signed, DaRqQuIEs, ſurgeon- major to the
Hoſpital.

OBSERVATIONS F Mr. DorLinoxn,

Surgeon- Major of the Royal Hoſpital at Graveline,
tranſmitted to the Court the 14 of April, 1757.

UPON the Court having done me the honour
to ſend me a letter from Mr. Goulard, ſurgeon-
major of the Royal Hoſpital at Montpellier, in
which the author makes public the compoſition of

his Bougies, whoſe baſis is the Extract of Saturn,

I carefully followed his directions with great
ſucceſs. ä

I was already acquainted with the virtues of the
Vegeto-mineral Water, by having made uſe of it
to great advantage in the cure of ulcers in the
legs, Which prove very obſtinate in this part of the
world. The following is the compoſition I have
made uſe of for theſe ſeventeen years paſt ; during
which time I have had the honour of being fur-
geon- major of this place,

Take four quarts of forge-water, two quarts of
good vinegar, and two pounds of litharge of gold:
bo the whole together down to five quarts
Then let it ſettle, and pour the liquor into tiles
well corked for uſe,

In regard to the Itech, upon Mr. De Chateau-

IT appears from the cures performed in
the above hoſpitals and thoſe in the military
one at Montpellier, wherein more than 2000
loldiers have made uſe of the Extract of Sa-
turn, that this medicine ought to be conſi-
dered as the true ſpecific for the treatment
of the Itch. In our firſt experiments, we
mixed it up with lard: but we are ſince
convinced by a multitude of facts, that a
mixture of the Extract of Saturn with com-
mon water, and a little brandy, is ſufficient
to cure the Itch, Nevertheleſs, to haſten

This method may be looked upon as the
moſt perfe& for the cure of the Itch, and
the moſt likely to anſwer the intentions of
the Miniſtry ; for in caſe it is followed, it
will not be neceſſary to ſend the ſoldiers
to the hoſpitals, which will be a conſiderable
ſaving to the King; ſeeing it will he ſuff.
cient to rub the patients with the Vegeto-
mineral Water at home.

Our remedy has this advantage in parti.
cular over others, that the fine particles, of
which it is compoſed, are more eafily intro-
duced into the ſource of the itchy leaven;
and conſequently we ſee the eruptions en-
creaſe from the fiſt day it is uſed, to the
fourth or fifth day, after which they dry
away. Mr. Imbert, Chancellor of the Uni-
verſity of Medicine, and InſpeQor of the Hol-
pitals, appears to be convinced, by the mul.
tiplication of the itchy eruptions, that thus

polite to repulſion, which has been 1njur-

FF. 149

of its ſucoeſs would fill many volumes;
ind I can ſay with great truth and ſatisfac-
tion, that is has been the means of removing
the prejuc ices of many celebrated phyſicians
and ſurgeons in this Univerlity, who were
formerly inclined to rank the Extract of Sa-
uin among repelling medicines. In a word,
it is the great external remedy uſed in this
city, ſo celebrated for the art of healing, as
well as in others, for the cure of external or
chirurgical diſeaſes, in which repellers muſt
be greatly hurtful; wheareas ours daily pro-
duces ſuch effects as ſurprize the maſters of
the art, who are not to be impoſed upon,
and were not convinced before they law a
thouſand proofs of its efficacy.

The ſame author in his Obſervations, to
decry the Extract of Saturn, has drawn an
argument from the accidents that happen to
the workmen in lead- mines: but though he
agrees with the reſt of mankind, in recom-
mending mercury as a ſpecific in the vene-
real diſeaſe, he takes no notice of the fatal
accidents to which the unhappy victims
condemned to labour in the quickſilver mines
are daily expoſed. It is neverthelels certain,
however good their conſtitutions may be, that
they never exceed four years, without being
ſeized with tremblings and contractions of
the nerves, involuntary ſpaſms of the muſ-
cles, habitual - and often mortal palſies.

is drawn the ſpecific for the venereal diſeaſe.
Now is it not evident, that the accidents
which befall the workmen employed in the
lead and quickfilver mines, are leſs the effects
of theſe minerals chan of the arſenical and
heterogeneous particles that are mixed with
them ?

Whoever would be convinced of the ex-
iſtence of thoſe heterogeneous ſubſtances,
need only take mercury, as it comes out of
the mine, and then pound it with a wooden
peſtle in a marble mortar full of water; and
it will be found, that a brown powder will
be ſeperated, and fall to the bottom of the
mortar, which will come away with the wa-
ter, when poured off.

Mercury, thus prepared, produces effects
known to all the world, by curing the mot
obſtinate venereal diſeaſes, without any lu-
conveniencies ever reſulting from it, when
adminiſtred by able hands. I do not exag-
gerate, when I aſſert, that I never loi: one
patient, amongſt the many thouſands that
I have attended in the Royal Hoſpital and
at home, by the bad effects of mercuty ;
which was undoubtedly owing to the atten-
tion I gave to the due preparation of 1t.

Now, do not the ſalutary effects produced
by mercury, nicely ſeperated from all hete-
rogeneous matter, authorize us to ſay, that
the ſame reſources may be found 1n the Pre-
parations of Lead for other complaints. if tne

like care is taken to have it well purife

before it is made uſe of? Such a preparation
is that which follows from an ebullition of
litharge with vinegar, of which the Extract
of Saturn is the reſult, employed under the
diferent forms we give it; and which per-
ſormes cures every day, which the moſt emi-
nent of the profeſſion cannot behold without
altoniſhment.

It muſt be obſerved, as an eſſential cir-
cumſtance, that the ſtrongeſt vinegar is the
moſt proper to diſſolve the litharge; on
which account we ſhall particularly recom-
mend that of Rouſſillon and Languedoc.

It is not leſs important to remark, that
the boiling of the litharge with the vinegar
ought to be continued an hour and a hall,
as I have mentioned in my letter to Mr.
De La Martiniere, dated in the year 1751;
and likewiſe in a Memoir read the ſame
year to the Royal Society at Montpellier.
An ounce of the Extract of Saturn and as
much brandy ſhould be put to a quart of
common water, which ought to be uſed, ge-
nerally ſpeaking, two or three times a day, in.
the appearance of the Itch, during the four
or five firſt days: But when the eruption
ſeems to be compleat, and tends toward a
turn by drying away, to every quart of
water, compoſed as above directed, ſhould
be added an ounce of ſea-ſalt, and an ounce
of allum in powder; which is to be uſed as
above, It may not be improper to obſerve,
that this addition is not abſolutely neceſſary

H 4 for

152 ATREATISE on LEAD,

for the cure of the Itch, and that it only
ſerves to accelerate it.

We ſhould add, that the action of our
remedy ought to be aſſiſted by a certain de-
gree of heat as was oblerved by the ſurgeon-
major of the hoſpital of Bethune : and as I
myſelf had formerly remarked to Mr. Imbert,
Inſpector of the Hoſpitals, and other phyſi-
cians and ſurgeons, who did me the h nour
to follow me in my vilitss This will not
appear ſurpriſing, if it be conſidered that the
principal effect of our remedy conliſts in
bringing out the itchy matter through the
pores of the ſkin; ſo that its action ought
naturally to be favoured by heat, to procure
an abundant tranſpiration.

However, I have not pretended to aſtert
that my medicine would cure the Itch when
combined with the venereal diſeaſe, king's
evil, or ſcurvy, as the author of the lall
Obſervations ſeems to inſinuate.

No body is ignorant, that theſe diſeaſes
require a particular method, and appropti-
ated medicines, There is room to judge
that this author is no practitioner, and that
he has been betrayed by weak authorities
into an eſteem of the remedies he propoſes.
Can he in effect blame the Extract of Saturn,
becauſe he believed it to be a repeller, while
he recommends an ointment made of allum
mixed with hog's lard ? But no body has the
leaſt doubt of allum being an aſtringent and

repeller, though perhaps they have doubted

Laſtly, it is with the Extract of Saturn
13 with mercury, and all other excellent re-

The difference in the time of curing the
Itch depends upon its quality, duration,
the number of puſtules, the ſtate of the
blood, complications, and, laſtly, the ſtate of
the weather,

H < In

154 A TREATISE on LEAD,

In general, the military aud puſtulous Itch
are cured in a very ſhort time.

The Itch in the beginning, where the
eruptions are not as yet entirely manifeſted,
requires ſome days more for a cure.

The ſcaly Itch takes a little more time in
the cure, becauſe it is not ſufficient to de-
ſtroy the cauſe. But we muſt alſo attend,
after the fall of the ſcales, to the reſtoration
of the epidermis.

The Itch that is at the ſame time ſcaly and
like a Tetter, requires ſtill more days; be-
cauſe we have not only the leaven of the
Itch to ſubdue, but alſo that of the Tetter.

If the venereal diſeaſe, ſcurvy, or the like
complaint, be complicated with the Itch, this
complication muſt be attended to, and muli
direct us in the method of cure.

When the blood of thoſe in the Itch is ex-
tremely heated, ſizy, and acrimonious, we
muſt be very liberal in the uſe of ſweeteners,
coolers, diluters, and domeſtic baths.

Experience has convinced us, that the
cold weather is not at all favourable to the
treatment of the Itch, becauſe it ſhuts up
the pores of the ſkin, and retards conle-
quently the tranſpiration of the itchy leaven,
when it is attenuated in the milatary glands
by the action of our metallic remedy. _

Although it may appear that patients in
the Itch were cured at Lille, in Flanders,
ſooner by the common method than by the

Extratt of Saturn; yet to decide equitably

were neither aſſiſted by a favourable ſeaſon

rooms of the patients were carefully warmed.
Without doubt, all theſe circumſtances were
more favourable in the other hoſpitals, where
the cure was effected in leſs time.

Whatever remedies are made uſe of for the
cure of the Itch, the ſame variations will oc-
cur, with this difference, that by the uſe of
our remedy the cure is ſooner compleated
(luppoling every other circumſtance parallel);
except when repellers are made uſe of, which,
by driving back the itchy leaven, cauſe the
cruptions to diſappear, and lay a foundation
for fatal conſequences : ſuch, for example, as

muſt be the effect of allum water, when made

ule of in the beginning of the diſeaſe,

. IX.
On Ruplures.

F all the branches of the art, there is

perhaps not one that has been carried
to that degree of perfection as the treatment
of Ruptures, and that ſo evidently demon-
ſtrates the ſuperior excellence of modern fur.
gery over that of the antients. I ſhall not
enter into a detail of the different kinds of
Ruptures, nor of their various cures. Such
as are deſirous to perfect themſelves in the
| knowledge of this important branch of the
profeſſion, may conſult the different authors
who have publiſhed very elaborate treatiſes
on this ſubje& ; but particularly the excel-
lent work of Mr. Arnaud, and the Memoirs
of the Royal Academy of Surgery. I hall
confine myſelf in this place to fome ſhot
remarks upon external applications, the na-
ture of the ſtrangled inteſtines, and the ope-
ration of reducing the inteſtine.

Belloſte is, I believe, the firſt who made
it appear, that emollients were hurtful in
Ruptures with ſtrangulation. He had, how-
ever, made uſe of them for thirty years, and
afterwards baniſhed both them and oily ap-
plications, which he looked upon, with reaſon

as pernicious remedies, I am of m_—
ow-

KV CTULELS ow

however, that the Vegeto-mineral Liquor, or
oxycrate, is preferable to ice and ſnow, which
are recommended by Belloſte.

With regard to Ane I am per-
ſuaded that when they begin there is no
inflammation, and that there only then exiſts
an inflation of that part of the gut which is
engaged in the ring. This inflation of the
gut is occaſioned by a rarefaction of the ſub-
ſtances confined in this portion of the inteſ-
tine; and which is the cauſe that the gut can-
not be puſhed back through the opening of
the ring, which now is become too mall.
This cannot remain long in this ſituation,
before the veſſels of the ſtrangled inteſtine
ſwell, and thence follows an inflammation, I
am not however of opinion, though it 1s ge-
nerally ſuppoſed, that the ring, which 1s
thought to be inflamed, contributes of itſelf
to the ſtrangulation. I rather look upon it,
in this caſe, as a cord upon the ſtretch, that
is not ſuſceptible of relaxation, whatever ap-
plications are employed to procure it.

By what I have ſaid, it is eaſy to perceive,
that we ought eſſentially to attend to a dimi-
nution of the ſize of the inteſtine; ſeeing it
is to the increaſed volume of it we are to
attribute the impoſlibiiity of returning it into
the abdomen. Now the remedies I have re-
commended above, I mean the Extract of
Saturn in Water, or oxycrate, are the moſt
proper to anſwer this indication; and the
ſhorteſt reflection is ſufficient to ſhew, a+

emol-

158 ATREATISE on LEAD.

emollient, by relaxing the coats of the in-
teſtine, cannot but render it more ſuſceptible
of extenſion, and conſequently favour the
ſtrangulation. | |

The taxis, or the operation by which we
reduce the inteſtine, is more difficult than is
imagined ; and I have ſeldom ſeen a ſurgeon
acquit himſelf with ſuccels, elpecially when
it is attended with ſtrangulation.

I ſhall now lay before the reader the man-
rer in which I have been accuſtomed to pro-
ceed in the reduction of the part. |

After having placed the patient in a proper
ſituation I put one hand under the thigh of
the affected fide, and apply the other upon
the tumour. Theſe two hands act in con-
junction by gentle and eaſy movements. The
air returns at fiiſt but in a little quantity,
and the ſwelling decreales but inſenſibly;
however, we muſt not be too haſty in the
operation, as we ſhould only put the patient!
to violent pain, without effetting a reduction
of the parts. But as the operation, performed
in the manner I have directed, laſts a cont
derable time, and very much fatigues the
ſurgeon, I am reheved occationally by al
alliſtant, to whom I recommend ſimply te
ſupport the parts in the ſame ſtate in which 1
left them. When 1 have recovered mylelt
from the fatigue, I return again to my work,
and go on in this method till I have made the
inteſtine recede. I can venture to lay, that
by proceeding thus with circumſp<ction [

The following Obſervations will help to

OBSERVATION I.

I WAS ſent for to Nimes, in the month of
July 1750, to ſee Mr. Teitic, Lord of Marguerite,
aged eighty-four, who for three days paſt had a
Rupture with ſtrangulation. The phyficians and
ſurgeons had applied the emollient cataplaſms ge-
nerally made uſe of on the occaſion. My firſt care
was to attempt the reduction of the inteſtine; but
not ſucceeding in my attempt, I replaced the for-
mer cataplaſm, and we conſulted on the moſt ad-
viſeable methods to be taken, to reſcue the patient
from the imminent danger he was expoſed to.
The gentlemen my aſſociates conſented to an ap-
plication of vinegar and water, for want of the
Extract of Saturn: one part of vinegar was mixed
to three parts of water. I caufed compreſſes, d ip-
ped in this mixture, to be applied to the tumour;
theſe were carefully moiſtened every half hour,
from eleven o'clock in the forenoon to ſix in the
afternoon ; at which time the gentlemen of the con-
ſultation returned. On removing the dreſſing, we
found the tumour diminiſhed one half, I made a
ſecond effort to reduce the parts, in which l ſuc-

ceeced, The patient cried out, I AM CURED
and he aQtually was ſo,

OBSERVATION I.

Communicated by Mr. Vivez, Maſter-Surgeon of this
4 ihe- Franche,

ON the 24th of April, 1758, I was ſent for to
a man who had an inguinal Rupture, with ſtran-
gulation, which I perceived to be only an entero.
celle. After having tried to reduce the part with-
out ſucceſs, by the application of the common re-
medies, ſuch as emollient cataplaſms and fomenta-
tions, I had at laſt recourſe to the Vegeto-mineral
Water. The effect of it was ſuch, that in fix
hours time the hernia receded of itſelf, and the
patient was perfectly cured,

OBSERVATION III.
By the ſame.

I WAS ſent for the 15th of July, 1759, toa

man who had a hernia, with ſtrangulation, joined
to a cirſocelle. Emollient cataplaſins and fomen-
tations, domeſtic baths, and other like remedics
generally preſcribed, had no effect. But by a
continued application of the Vegeto-mineral Wa—
ter for eight or ten hours, the p:rts retreated, and
the patient found great relief from the cirlocclic
for a long time after.

ſion of the lower belly, with a vomiting of chylous

matter, which had continued for three days. te

G. X.
On the Piles.

T is well known that, the Piles are ſwel-

lings of the veins, which ſurround the
anus. This diſeaſe is produced by a vilci-
dity of the blood and lymph, an obſtruc-
tion in ſome of the inteſtines of the lower
belly, or an irritation therein, occaſioned by
the excrements being very hard, and of an
acrimonious diſpoſition,

This hardneſs is ſo conſiderable in ſume
ſubjects, that the fœces may be conſidered,
in the cfforts that are made to go to ſtool,
as a ſolid wedge, which is ſtrongly puſhed
downwards by the combined action of the
midriff and muſcles which ſurround the ab-
domen, and which acts with violence upon
the ſides of the rectum, and forces into the
hæmorrhoidal veſſels a ſuper-abundance of
blood, which inſenſibly extends them very
conſiderably. The Piles either appear exter-

nally, or are confined within; which dif-

ference has occaßoned their being called ex-
ternal or internal. I have ſeen the laſt of an
enormous ſize, and put the patient to as great
pain as a woman ſuffers in labour, and that
as often as he went to ſtool.

DURING

164 A TREATISE on LEAD.
DURING my ſtay at Genoa, I was ſent for to

Padre Cuneo, a religious 3 and Divine to the
Archbiſhop of that city. He had the internal Piles,
which he was obliged to puſh out every time he

went to ſtool; and it is difficult to expreſs what |

he ſuffered, both when they came out and receded,
He could never be prevailed upon to ſubmit to the
operation; however, upon my repreſenting to him
the neceſſity of it, he ſubmitted to it, and it was
performed after the ordinary preparations in the
following manner.

The Piles being forced outwards, I ſeized the
packet, which was very large, with my hand, and
cut off above half of them, The reaſon why !
did not cut them all off, was to obviate the fol-
lowing inconveniences : It appears from experi-
ence, that when the whole packet is cut off by a
circular ſtroke, the ſcar being contracted, and dif-
ficult to ſubdue, ſtops up the paſſage through
which the excrements paſs, as I have often had
opportunities to obſerve. Befides, one need not
be uneaſy on account of the remaining part of
the Piles, as by the flux of blood they die away;
and this favours the dreſſing, as it does the placing
a roll made of many pieces of lint faſtened together
above the parts that were cut. The operation
finiſhed, the patient was dreſſed according to art:
two-hours after, he had ſo great an inclination to
go. to ſtool, that the dreſſings were obliged to be
taken off, and ſo violent an hæmorrhage ſucceed-
ed, that great danger might have enſued, had they
not ſent for me immediately to ſtop it, It was
methodically dreſſed, and the patient was in a fair

way of recovery at my departure from Genoa, in

Ottober 1748.

I ſhall not here relate any more Obſerva-
tions upon the Piles, becauſe there is no
practitioner who has not ſeen many caſes of
this kind, and 1s not convinced that the
Piles are ſuſceptible of prodigious magnitude,
when precautions are not taken to hinder it,
To anſwer this purpole, the ule of ſweateners,
and a proper regimen, is neceſſary; as are
likewiſe gliſters, to prevent the irritations oc-
caſioned by the hardneſs of the fœces, as
often as it is neceſſary to go to ſtool.

When the glyſter is come away, I waſh the
Piles with the Vegeto-mineral Water, and
then apply to them the Cerate, made with
wax, oil, and the above Water. If the Piles
are external, I waſh them many times a-da
with the Water, and then apply the Cerate.
This method of treating the Piles has ſuc-
ceeded ſo well with me, that if I had collected
all the caſes wherein it had anſwered my ex-

— the detail would be aſſuredly very
ong.

168 A TREATISE on LEAD.

Another Letter from the ſame.

ASK me no longer for any farther proofs of the

excellency of your remedy ; it is beyond all doubt:
you may have as many certificates as you pleaſe,
concerning its miraculous effects. It is the true
chirurgical panacea, There 1s no regiment with-
out it, and in which it is not uſed with great ſuc-
ceſs, for the diſperſing of ſwellings and inflam-
mations, even ſuch as threaten a gangrene. I hare
always found it of very great ſervice 1n the moſt
deſperate caſes, uſed in baths, lotions, and pump-
ings. All my brethren, to whom I have mentioned
the remedy, have related inſtances of its amazing
efficacy: and chiefly our friend Mr. Cremoux, to
whole merit you are no ſtranger, He has often re-
lated to me the cures performed either by himſelf
or father; but they are too numerous to recapitu-
late here: however, they are certainly not only a
juſt eulogium, but a convincing proof of the efficacy
of your remedy. The chirurgical art owes you it
befl wiſhes, and its moſt grateful acknowledge-
ments. You have been able to new-modity an
old remedy, and to render it uſeful in every com-
plaint; a thing never known before, I am, &&.

Extract of a Letter from Mr.. ALVNGRI, Surgeon-
Major of Fort Breſcou.

I HAVE long, Sir, obſerved the admirable ef-
{es of the Extract of Saturn, The cures I have
performed by its means are too numerous to be par-
ticularized here. I ſhall therefore only obſerve 1n
general, that I have ſeldom met with a caſe in
which it has not ſucceeded, either as a lotion, po-
matum, or mixed with digeſtives. I have known
it perform wonders in inflammations, wounds, ul-
cers, &e. But what has made me an enthuſiaſt in
its favour, if I may ſo ſpeak, is, that I have cured
occult cancers in their beginning, by the applica-
tion of it.

Signed ALYNGRI.

Extract of a Letter from Mr LAUMONT, Surgeon
of the Royal Marines,

THE proofs which I have of the good effects
of the Extract of Saturn authorize me to aſſure
the Public, that it may be looked upon as a ſpecific
in all chirurgical diſeaſes, which do not take their
riſe from any fault in the fluids. I have uſed it
with ſucceſs in the cure of tetters. and the itch,
contuſions and ſprains, of all kinds of Ulcers, phy-
moſis and paraphymoſis, deafneſs, opihalmics, and
theumatic pains, For all theſe complaints, I have
uſed it in lotions, fomentations, pumpings, in-
jections and cataplaſms : In teſtimony of which I
lave ſigned this certificate.

LAUMONT.

Extract of a Letter from Mr. FELIx, Surgeon-
Major of a Man of War, the Zoth of July,
1754+

Mr. Felix is a Creole, native of the Ha-
vannah ; was apprentice to me, and then
ſerved 1n the Military Hoſpital here as under-
ſurgcon. T he following is the Extract of a

Letter he wrote to me on has return from the
Indies.

I CAN aflure you with equal ſatisfaction and
gratitude, that in my arſt voyage to Vera-Cruz I
was compleatly happy. I performed many fur-
priſing operations, and cured many chirurgical
diforders, the account of which would be tedious.
All that I ſhould ſay, Sir, is that I have often made
uſe of your remedy, and even for the dreſſings
aſter operations performed. Ihe patients were ſoon
relieved from pain, and ſo ſoon cured, that they
could not ſay enough in commendation of your
| admirable Water, l have employed it with equal
ſacceſs at the Havannah, and acquired by it a re-
putation above all my brethren, who were witneſſes
of its efficacy.

A Letter from Mr. PECHAGUT, an old Officer,

SIR,
I PERFORM miracles with the Extract of

Saturn, My door is continually crowded with poor |
invalids, and I have the ſatisfaction of ſeeing them
come to thank me, perfectly recovered. But I
have been much ſurpriſed lately by the cure [ |
performed on a child, who had a cancer on his
cheek; to which I applied the pure Extract, and
afterwards ſome butter, which had been waſhed in
the Vegeto-mineral Water. In twelve days time
the wound was healed, to the great farpeih of us
dabblers in phyſic.
