An OBSERVATION communicated ly
Mr. SALANSONE, M. B.

ABOUT the middle of June, 1760, a friend
of mine was afflicted with two tetters, one of
which occupicd the inferior part of the right omo-
plata, and the other the left hypochondrium ; they
were both an aſſemblage of ſmall pimples, very
much inflamed, covering about the breadth of a
crown-piece, and placed in ſuch a manner, that
each tetter, eſpecially the one of the hypochon-
drium, deſcribed pretty near a circle, The itching
they brought on was inſupportable, and in conje-
quence of the leaſt ſcratching, the heat of them was
conſiderably augmented : they likewiſe gave ite to
occaſional thivering fits. From theſe ſymptoms !
concluded that they were malignant tetters, par-
taking a good deal of the nature of an erißpelas,

two very intereſting ſubjects. Firſt, it proves |

A GIRL of a hectic and bilious temperament |

was ſuddenly ſeized with a ſharp pain in one of her
feet. The complaint in the beginning was ſup-
portable, but ſoon encreaſed to ſuch a degree as

forced her to cry out. She ſuffered excruciating |}

pains for ſome hours, before I was ſent for, My
firſt care was to examine the part affected, which

to the eye appeared in its nataral ſound ſtate: |

tention or ſtiffneſs, however, might be felt: I aſked
her to what ſhe could attribute the cauſe of her
complaint, but could get no information from her,
The uneaſy ſituation of the patient did not admit
of any farther enquiry or deliberation ; and [I haſt-

ened to relieve her by the moſt aſſuaging appli- |

cations. To this purpoſe J ordered the part af-
fected to be rubbed with the ointment of marſh-
mallows, to which 1 added ſoon after the oils of
ſweet almonds and camomile. Notwithſtanding

the uſe of this linament, the pain continued equally |

ſevere, and rather ſeemed to encreaſe. I then ſub-
ſtituted ſome ſoftening balſam in the room of the
ointment of marſh-mallow, but ſtill with the ſame
ſucceſs, The patient perceiving no relief from any
of the applications, began to be greatly alarmed,
and deſired ver earneftly I would give her ſome-
thing more efficacious.

of doing, by the addition of ſome anodyne drops to

opiates internally. Bleeding might have been ad-

ticular reaſons forbid it: I ordered her, in conſe-
quence, a narcotic julep; but ftill no alteration
could be perceived for the better: ſuch was the
pecular nature of the pain, that the remedies
proper on ſuch occaſions, ſo far from contributing
to mitigate it, ſerved only to encreaſe its violence.
What I had applied, hower, were the only topi-
cal remedies which appeared, as things were cir-
cumſtanced, adviſeable. The pain could be miti-
vated by anodynes only, The ſtiffneſs obſerveable
on the part, which was the effect of a ſpaſm, as I
ſhall prove further on, ſeemed to authorize the
application of emollients; and the intenſeneſs of
the pain could not admit of any medicine, which
did not poſſeſs the anodyne quality in the higheſt
degree. The inefficacy of this laſt remedy, I mean
the narcotic, threw me, I own, into the greateſt
conſternation ; and whilſt I was deliberating on
the next ſtep which would be proper to take upon
the occaſion, I luckily thought of trying Mr. Gou-

The caſe above related is a ſtriking inſtance
of the anodyne virtue of Mr. Goulard's remedy ;
and, in effect, is it not greatly anodyne, fince it
{ucceeded in a caſe where narcotics, which are
looked upon as ſpecifics when properly applied, ſo
far from mitigating the pain, only ferved to en-
creaſe it? May we rot be allowed then to call it
more ſpecific than ſpecifics themſelves ? Thongh
this propoſition may in general be looked upon
erroneous, it certainly can by no means be ſo in
the caſe we have juſt mentioned; Mr. Goulard 1
convinced by a multiplicity of experiments, that
there is no topical medicine more effectual for al-
laying inflammatory fevers than his Vegeto-mine-
neral Water. The fact I have juſt related ſerves
to prove it no leſs efficacious in eaſing ſuch as do
not ariſe from inflammation z moreover, it prove
it to poſſeſs a real antiſpaſmodic quality.

What I have juſt advanced is eattly demon-
ſtrated ; which J hope to do by ſhewing, that this
Pain entirely proceeded from a ſpaſmodic aftection-

The bad effects of the anodynes, which had
ee the application of the Vegeto- mineral
Vater, and the ſpaſmodic nature of the complaint,
which had yielded to that medicine, ſerve to con-
firm a propoſition of Mr. Goulard's, which will
no doubt appear an extraordinary one to thoſe who
are governed by prejudice, and pay a greater de-
ference to old errors than any modern matters of
fi&, But to come to the point. Mr. Goulard,
in the excellent Treatiſe he is going to publiſh, is
not afraid to advance, that the uſe of emollients
for removing inflammatory tumours is attended
with the worſt conſequences. Every one knows
the danger of applying ſuch medicines in an eryfi-
pelas, though looked upon advantageous in phleg-
monous inflammations. Mr. Goulard, notwith-
ſtanding, is well convinced by repeated Obſerva-
tions, that they are no leſs dangerous in the latter.
Here theory coincides with practice. In effect, the
cauſe of tumours being nothing more than the force
of the fluids being ſuperior to the reſiſtance of
the ſolids, it is evident, that any thing which
ſerves to weaken the latter, while the force of the
former remains in the ſame degree, muſt neceſ-
ſarily either occaſion a tumour, or add to its vo-

lume, if already exiſting. Now ſuch appear to be
tus

the effects of emollients; it is therefore with rea-

treatment of tumours, whether phlegmonous, eri- |

fipelatous, or inflammatory. Let us next ſee from
the caſe J have related, whether we cannot bring a
freth proof of the danger of applying emollients for
che cure of inflammatory tumours. If theſe appli-
cations can ever be preper in ſuch caſes, it muſt

flamed part, But even in ſuch a caſe there may be

Obſervation. We may then from this draw a very
ſafe and concluſive argument, That fince emol-
hents proved very pernicious 1n a caſe where they
are principally indicated, their uſe ought to be ab-
lolutely baniſhed in the treatment of inflammatory
tumours, as Mr, Goulard remarks.

guous and ulcerated, were very diſagreeable to the
eye, and very troubleſome to the patient. It was
ſix months ſince the eruption firſt appeared; the
ointment of ſulphur had been often uſed without
